The body of Marvin Hayden was discovered in his
own library, dead from a bullet wound in his head. The
pistol was undoubtedly his. The Medical Examiner tes-
tified that the shot could have been fired by his own
hand.
But the old butler, Gillespie, scoffs at the idea of sui-
cide. He knows what he knows. "... Caught red-
handed. Yes! The two of them together. And blood
on his hands! Let them squirm and lie. The truth will
out. Even if Mr. Valentine did drag in the smart red-
headed detective, Mr. Peter Clancy. But of course he
wanted to protect his sister.

Would You Like to Know
how RED BADGE books are selected?
Each year, hundreds of detective-story manuscripts
are submitted to the literary editors of Dodd, Mead
and Company. From these only a very small number—
ten or twelve at most-receive the coveted Red Badge
imprint. This is because every detective story that car-
ries the Red Badge must first pass a rigid eight-point
test-a test so severe that all but absolutely first-class
detective stories are quickly eliminated.
If you would like to receive this eight-point test
which is used by the literary editors of Dodd, Mead
and Company in selecting Red Badge detective stories,
we will be glad to mail you a copy without charge. Send
your name and address to
DODD, MEAD & COMPANY, INC.
Department RB
449 Fourth Avenue, New York

SUDDEN DEATH

CHAPTER I
"I KILLED Marvin Hayden."
The words forced their way through clenched teeth.
The man's narrow, highbred face was thrust forward. His
breath came unevenly. There was a strange, almost
fanatic glitter in his eyes.
In blank amazement, Captain of Detectives Kerrigan
stared across his office desk. He glanced at the small
correct bit of engraved pasteboard that lay upon his
blotter.
"Mr. Douglas Evarest," he read aloud. "Do you mean
me to believe—”
"I have waited too long already." The man's thin sen-
sitive hands were pressed down hard and flat on the pol-
ished oak of the desk top. The arms looked as rigid as
rods of iron. He cleared his throat as if it hurt and added
with somber violence, "I am giving myself up. Surely I
make it plain."
"But, Mr. Evarest-" Kerrigan rose slowly to his
feet. His swivel chair oscillated queerly with the uncer-
tainty of the movement-"I can't understand. It doesn't
seem reasonable. I mean, you know, the only information
the police have in regard to you is that you played bridge
at the Haydens' night before last. Gillespie, the butler,
told us that, but there was nothing-"
"How could Gillespie know that I came back Sunday
I
2
SUDDEN DEATH
night-later-and killed Hayden?" Evarest spoke with
a strange sort of desperate clarity.
"Killed him? How?" Kerrigan's glance was cool and
incisive.
"Shot him. With his own pistol." The answer came
promptly but Evarest's lips were dry, and as he closed
them in a hard straight line, the muscles of his throat
contracted visibly.
"You could have read those facts in the papers yester-
day or this morning, Mr. Evarest." Kerrigan's tone was
grave but curiously gentle. "The district attorney's office
isn't having any secret sorrows about this case. You
could also have read of the arrest-" He suddenly
reached out, switched on all the lights and turned to fix
the other with his keen, slightly bulging eyes. "You're
maybe a friend of young James Hayden?" he asked
quietly.
Evarest started back. "I-why, yes. Of course. I-"
He stopped suddenly, with a strange, angry, baffled ges-
ture.
"In which case," said Kerrigan, still mildly, "if you
really are guilty of the murder of his father, do you mind
telling me why you have let so much time go by before
you showed up here?"
"Put it down to cowardice if you like," said Evarest
with a bitter half smile.
Kerrigan studied the man's scholarly, determined face.
"Maybe you were putting your house in order as they say
in books. Was that it?"
"If you prefer," Evarest agreed. "I had some neces-

sary arrangements to-"
"Wait a second." Kerrigan threw up his hand impa-
tiently, for the buzzer on his desk was sounding with im-
perative intermittent insistence. "Take a chair, Mr.
Evarest. I'll be with you- What?" He spoke into the
instrument. "Oh. Well, tell him- No. Put him on."
There was a slight pause, then Kerrigan said, "No.
You don't mean it! Well . . . I'm not sure. Maybe-
Where are you? Oh. I might have known. And the
kid with you? . . ." An instant of silence in which the
police detective chuckled grimly. “Oh, yes, I know
you're the original little Bright Eyes-and perhaps―
All right, your honor. Maybe I could use an extra pair
of brains. Come on in. What? Bring the kid? Hold
up. I'm not so sure. Wait a second."
Not for an instant had Kerrigan's searching glance
wavered from the tense strained face of the self-accused
murderer who fronted him grimly. Now he dropped his
voice. "Mr. Evarest."
"Yes?" The man's eyes seemed to focus with diffi-
culty.
"Did you ever hear of a chap-name of Peter Clancy?"
Evarest shook his head. "I don't-don't think so.

"He's a private detective. Not on the force, you un-

"But I don't need or want-"
"Just so." Kerrigan nodded wisely. "But I do. See?
I've never found it profitable to turn down Peter Clancy,
so if you don't mind I'd like to have him in. He's out-

side now."
Evarest frowned. "I suppose it must be," he said dully.
"Whatever you say. I'm ready."
Kerrigan lifted the telephone instrument and then low-
ered it again, keeping it in his hand. "What do you
know about young Berkley, Mr. Evarest?" he asked
hastily.
"Val? Mrs. Hayden's brother Valentine?"
"Yes. Must be an enterprising kid. Seems he's en-
gaged Clancy's services. He's out there with him now."
"Not here!"
"Yes. Clancy wants to bring him in. Do you mind?"
The face of Douglas Evarest was like a white mask
through which dark burning eyes gazed blankly.
"It must be, I suppose. What does it matter?" he said
hoarsely.
Kerrigan spoke a few words into the telephone. A
minute later the door opened.
"How are you, Jake? Oh, I beg your pardon."
Kerrigan watched the strong homely face of his un-
official colleague with eager interest and some amuse-
ment. It was good to see Peter Clancy taken aback.
The identity of Kerrigan's visitor had been made in-
stantly plain, for young Berkley had spoken the other
man's name in a startled tone as he came through the
door.
"Douglas Evarest! What are you doing here?" The
young voice was rough with apprehension. "Did Emily
send you? Has anything new happened? Oh, God . .
He came forward, his face, sensitive, untried and undis-

ciplined, working oddly.
Kerrigan's voice sounded firm and unemotional as hard
rubber. "Mr. Evarest, my-er-unofficial friend, Peter
Clancy. Sit down, Mr. Berkley. We have some interest-
ing news."
"Did Emily send you, Douglas?" insisted Valentine
Berkley. "For Heaven's sake, tell me what's happened."
"You're worried about-your sister, Mr. Berkley?" ob-
served Kerrigan, glancing keenly aside at Peter Clancy.
"Naturally he would be," Peter answered for his new
client. "The shock of her husband's death was a terrible
thing for Mrs. Hayden."
"She was bearing it O.K. when I saw her yesterday,"
Kerrigan said, not unkindly, though Clancy thought he
detected a hint of hidden sarcasm. "Your sister has good
strong nerves, Berkley. Not many women as young as
she is could show so much self-control through a long,
grilling day, and after such a night.”
Douglas Evarest had dropped back into his chair. He
was clutching the arms. His head was thrust slightly
forward and he coughed twice without seeming to know
that he had done so. His strained febrile glance flashed
from one face to the other without an instant's pause.
Clancy bent his red head as he carefully ground out the
end-of a cigarette in Kerrigan's ample ash-tray, but his
steel-blue glance took in every detail of the scene.
"You won't find many girls like my sister," Berkley
retorted, sharp upon the end of Kerrigan's remark.
"She's Oh, well. You wouldn't understand." The
boy was in his early twenties and might well be forgiven

the emotion he apparently could not entirely control. He

"That's all right, Berkley," Clancy interrupted quietly.
"Captain Kerrigan has some news for us already, I think.
Something that hasn't gotten into the papers, Jake?"
"Well-er-yes." Kerrigan glanced aside uncertainly.
"And for the benefit of all concerned I propose we keep

the district attorney's office didn't get wise until-well-
until we've had time to check up a bit."
"Looks like Updegraff sure was trying to make char-
acter with the proletariat on this case," remarked Peter
Clancy. "For an assistant district attorney, he's made a
big enough splash on the front page to-day to suggest he
might have political ambitions. Been stealing your thun-
der, Jake?"
In Kerrigan's throat some indistinguishable profanity
rumbled, then he said: "It's not my habit, as you're well
aware, Pete, to invite the press to take orchestra seats
before we know what the play's about. But Mr. Upde-
graff and that man of his, Inverness, have their own
methods. Inverness has talked aplenty and so has Up-
degraff. And even before the medical officer got his re-

"Well, but of course," exclaimed Clancy, studying the

"No," said Evarest hoarsely. "Nothing since-" He

"Tell me. Why I mean- Do you think

"That James Hayden did not shoot his own father in
his own home night before last," Kerrigan prompted with
a curious half smile.
Evarest glanced uncertainly at the police detective and
then swiftly toward the able private investigator. "Yes,"
he said. "Why do you think it might-was-suicide, Mr.
Clancy?"
"Because the report of the medical officer is to the
effect that Marvin Hayden could perfectly well have fired
the shot that killed him." Peter was acutely conscious
of the sharp intake of Evarest's breath and the slow re-
laxed exhalation that inaudibly followed. He went on
without pause. "The medical examiner from the district
attorney's office also had to admit that it was at least a
8
SUDDEN DEATH
physical possibility."
"I was sure of that much from the first," said Kerrigan,
addressing Clancy, but with an oddly compounded side
glance at Douglas Evarest. "Whatever Updegraff may
contend, the fact remains that the body of Marvin Hay-
den was discovered in the man's own library, and that
the weapon used, according to the ballistician, was un-
questionably his. There are witnesses to prove that the
pistol had fallen within reach of his hand. He was shot
at close range . . . through the head. From the
nature of the wound"-Kerrigan's expression was grim
with the details he withheld-"no powder marks would
have been visible in any case."
"It did-really-look like suicide?" gasped Evarest,
white to the lips.
"And if it did, Mr. Evarest"-with a thrill of excite-
ment Kerrigan watched Peter's intent face as he sprung
his trap-"will you still assert that it was you who killed
Marvin Hayden?"

"Yes. Yes, Valentine. I know," Evarest interrupted
thickly. He looked as if he were about to faint.
Clancy put his hand on the shoulder of his young client.
"Mr. Evarest is a great friend of your family, isn't he,
Berkley?"
The boy looked up into Peter's serious, clever face.
"Yes," he answered at once.
"A very close and old friend of James Hayden-and
of your sister?”
Valentine's dark brows drew together sharply. "Of
James Hayden. Yes. Of course. We-my sister and I
haven't known him so long. Only since she married-Mr.
Hayden. About two years ago. But we are all—very—
good friends." He spoke slowly, staring down at Douglas
Evarest's bent head.
"Maybe I ought to say in so many words"-Kerrigan
looked from the boy to Clancy and then back again-
"that Mr. Evarest came in here of his own accord a few
minutes ago, and in no uncertain terms confessed to the
murder of Marvin Hayden."
"But why-" began Valentine.

SUDDEN DEATH
"Skip it for the minute," Peter interrupted crisply but
with an apologetic gesture. "I'd like to ask Mr. Evarest
a question, Jake. May I?"
"Sure." Kerrigan nodded.
"Did you know, Mr. Evarest, that even when a man
confesses to a crime it is necessary that he prove his
statements?"
"Why?" Evarest bit his lip as he bent his startled
glance on the face of the red-headed detective. "In a
case as serious-as-as murder-I should think it would
be enough-"
"But it isn't." Kerrigan spoke definitely. "You see,
Mr. Evarest, a good many of us have heard of you in
various ways. It's quite easy to understand that your
researches have been along very different lines from what
Clancy's and mine have been, and that it is quite likely
you weren't wise on the point that Clancy has just brought
up. Now that it has been brought up,” he repeated, “how
do you propose to prove that it was you who shot Mr.
Hayden night before last?"
“I—" Evarest coughed, and had to begin again.
"Wouldn't a description-of it-be enough?"
"It would have to contain facts that had not appeared
in the papers." Peter spoke with grave insistence. "And
if, Mr. Evarest, it turns out, as seems quite possible ac-
cording to the last accounts, that the presumption of
suicide is sufficiently strong to clear James Hayden when
he appears in the Homicide Court to-morrow morning—
would you then wish your accusation of yourself, just
made to Captain Kerrigan, to stand?"

II
"Suicide," Evarest exclaimed in a hushed voice. "If

"Then you withdraw the confession you just made to

yours is one of the cases that makes it necessary to get
more proof than a man's own word for his having com-
mitted a crime. We're in our jobs to see that justice
doesn't miscarry, not even when"-he cleared his throat
-"when a chap would make a try at giving his life for
a friend."
"But you mustn't-you mustn't think that," Evarest
broke in uncertainly.
"If you believe that there's a chance it can be proved
to be a case of suicide," Kerrigan retorted shrewdly, "it's
a cinch you didn't fire the shot yourself, Mr. Evarest. A
child could see that. It's clear enough why you said you
did. You see why, don't you, Mr. Berkley?"
The boy's extraordinarily handsome face had gone from

CHAPTER III
PETER CLANCY stepped into the waiting limousine. Berk-
ley followed. A handsome young chauffeur in spotless
fawn-colored livery closed the door. The front glass was
tightly shut so that Peter was, to all intents and purposes,
alone with his young client. As they started, the detec-
tive sank back among the luxurious broadcloth cushions
and observed carelessly: "Your brother certainly did him-
self well in the matter of cars. I don't know when I've
seen anything richer or more beautiful than this one."
"Brother?" Berkley instinctively repudiated the im-
plied relationship. "Mr. Hayden had no taste. I picked
out the upholstery and the ebony gadgets to go with some
sables and things of Emily's."
"Oh," said Peter quietly. "Then it's your sister's
car?"
"Yes. A Christmas present. Not last Christmas.
She's had it over a year. Getting on for two."
"It still seems in a fairly good state of repair for such
an antique." Peter responded to the scorn in the boy's
voice rather than to the words. "Mr. Hayden- You
still called him that?"
Berkley nodded shortly.
"To his face?"
"Yes." The reply was sharp and bitter. "Why not?
He was years older. Fifty-eight. I think I told you."
18
SUDDEN DEATH
19
"And your sister isn't so very much older than you.
Twenty-nine you said. . . ." Peter, quietly meditative.
"He would have been very much in love with her no
doubt."
The boy's averted face was sullen. "He was."
Peter felt the words to be significant. The use of the
past tense meant more than a reference to the immediate
past he shrewdly inferred, but he made no such observa-
tion aloud. Rather, he repeated the words, guilefully ac-
centing the pronoun as if he had understood the other to

Peter was familiar (as what dyed-in-the-wool New
Yorker would not be?) with the imposingly restrained
façade of the old Marvin mansion that had come down to
the late incumbent through his mother's family, together
with the scarcely less impressive line of ships that had
-founded the family fortune in the days when steam was
little more to the towering East Indiamen than a pleasing
accompaniment to the tea which was one of their reasons
for being. With the coming of steam and the develop-
ment of South American trade the old romance of the sea
had materially abated, and in Marvin Hayden the chief
interest in the present line of coastwise freighters had
22
SUDDEN DEATH
been wholly financial. This much was common property.
The extent of the fortune that his astuteness had amassed
was understood to be well up in the nine-figure class.
As Peter watched Valentine Berkley's nervous and im-

vast fortune would be divided and what changes would
come to the stately old house in which Marvin Hayden
was born-and in which his shattered and defaced body
had been found.

CHAPTER IV
PETER sent Valentine Berkley to take Evarest's flowers
to Mrs. Hayden and to inquire whether she would not
grant him a short interview. Believing that the boy had
already imparted all that he knew-or was willing to tell
-of the tragedy, Peter felt freer in his absence. Almost
immediately thereafter the detective went out into the
hall where he found Murphy in earnest converse with a
slight inconspicuous man in a superlatively tailored chauf-
feur's uniform. At Clancy's appearance, like magic the
smart military cap and long braided top-coat were re-
moved and the man stood forth in the correct indoor garb
of the upperest of upper servants. Neither the long coat
nor the charmingly striped trousers that it served almost
to hide were, to the chauffeur side of Wiggar, an answer
to prayer. Only his devotion to his master made the
compromise possible and he never laid aside the slightly
incongruous portions of his habiliments without a touch-
ing though silent pean of thanksgiving. As he appeared
now, so would he fain appear, if convenient to an all-
wise judge, on the last great day. With the dignified
consciousness of a perfection that, like a good wine,
needed no bush, he approached his master. "I think I
have everything you may require, Mr. Peter." He pro-
duced an elegantly finished pig-skin bag that he had mo-
mentarily deposited in the shadow of a large chair.
26
SUDDEN DEATH
27
The young footman who had responded with consid-

of the lower floor had determined the fact that a spacious
dining-room occupied nearly the whole of the rear and
was reached through arched double-doors opening into
the back part of the drawing-room, as well as through
an inconspicuous one in the dark panelling of the hall
behind the foot of the great staircase. From the size of
the other apartments Peter concluded that there probably
was but one more on this level: the library: the room in
which Mr. Hayden had been found dead.
From the moment of his entrance, like a lode-stone,
this library had drawn his thoughts. Here was to be the
beginning, if not the end, of his quest. He now had a
pretty shrewd idea of what was supposed to be required
of him, though it had not been put to him in so many
words. But Peter Clancy took no man's money unless
he was able to give entire satisfaction, and at this min-

Peter was never satisfied until he had seen as much as
possible of the terrain involved in any given campaign.
He may have had additional reasons for self-congratula-

which Berkley received his suggestion, the young man
led the way up through the silent house.
Through the rear of the hall and up the great staircase
their feet fell noiselessly on deep-piled carpets. The pas-
sage at the top was wide but shorter than would have
been expected for the rooms at front and back were deep
and extended across the width of the house.
"One more flight," Valentine said in a hushed voice.
He looked back across his shoulder. Peter noticed that
the boy's glance shrank away from the closed door on
his right, and guessed rightly what awful thing lay be-
yond there in a darkened room.
Peter stopped. "I'll go in here," he said with an air
of authority that could not easily be denied.
Young Berkley shuddered away with the horrified eyes

"It was suicide. You understand? You are clever
enough to understand and realize that it could have
been only that. Only that. Jim couldn't- Couldn't!-"
In mercy Peter turned away from the look on her
ravaged face.
CHAPTER VI
"BE sure it's Gillespie that brings them, and when he
comes ask him to wait." Peter's tone was in no sense
dictatorial but simply took a prompt response for granted.
He leaned back in his chair, and while Valentine spoke
over the house telephone he took a swift but intensely
interested survey of the room.
The furnishings, Peter rightly surmised, had been left
to the boy's taste, and gave to this earnest student of
human nature another side light on the characteristics of
his young client. It was a bed-sitting room, but the old-
fashioned homely name for it was singularly out of place.
The walls were hung with a paper covered with huge
white calla lilies, shaded in silver gray and silver green.
On the floor was a spacious rug in various tones of white
with a queer, intricate border of a spirited mauve and
turquoise. Thin curtains of lemon tissue veiled the fall-
ing night, and framing the three big windows were dra-
peries of brocade that harmonized the light and vivid
colors of curtain and rug. Opposite the door into the hall,
on a low dais, was a bed arranged in the Empire manner
with the side toward the wall. It was hung with the
same brocade as the windows, the long, shining folds
flowing down the steps to the floor. Near the head, on a
carved alabaster bracket, was a small figure in marble,
exquisite in workmanship but so sternly restrained in its

In all the pale, softly shining room, this point of light
caught and held Peter's attention. He was staring to-

"What is it called-the statue?" Peter asked without

"Beauty, it is to me." The boy's full lips moved

As if irresistibly drawn, Peter rose and moved toward

and Truth," he said.
"For me Beauty is enough," the boy's tone was unex-
pectedly and sharply defensive.
Peter smiled. "So, like a true devotee, you keep a
perpetual light burning at her shrine."
"Is there any harm in that?" Valentine retorted.
"None," said Peter. "But is it really a fact? Do you
keep a light burning here day and night?"
"Yes. What of it?"
"Nothing," Peter answered cheerfully. "A pretty idea
I think. But doesn't it keep you awake?"

Peter heard Valentine take in a quick, harsh breath,

CHAPTER VII
PETER paced slowly the length of the long room and back
again. He stopped in front of Valentine Berkley and put-
ting both strong hands upon the boy's shoulders forced
him to look up.
"What is this black trouble that Gillespie talks about?"
he asked with kind but stern insistence. "If you expect
me to be of the slightest use I must know."
Valentine shifted from one foot to the other and his
eyes were anywhere but on Peter's face. He cleared his
throat and wet his lips before he could speak. "Hayden
-Mr. Hayden-was a jealous man. Frightfully jealous."
"Of whom?"
""
"Of everyone who loves her. Everyone. Even the
girls she is fond of. Even me. And Williams and Evar-
est and Charlie Fosdick and-"
"And James Hayden?" Peter's hands were like steel
clamps on the boy's shoulders. He paused a breathless
instant and then went sternly on. "Was your sister's
husband jealous of his own son?”
"He had no reason to be. That I swear." The young
face looked white-hot. "Emily has stood- Oh, you
don't know-you couldn't imagine what she's had to
stand, Clancy! Why, all that evening when we were
playing bridge-the hateful, sarcastic things he said to
her and to me!"

"Mr. James Hayden was not here at that time?" Peter

"Don't be absurd," said Peter quietly. "A telegram-

fully. "We've never had any secrets-not since I was
a-just a baby."
Peter repressed an inward smile of scepticism and
changed his line of inquiry to approach from another
angle. "Since you and your sister are so close you would
know all about her friends. Were any of these men that
you mentioned-Fosdick, Evarest, Williams-in love with

"No one could know Emily well and not love her,"
said her brother with strong conviction. "But that's not
necessarily what one means by being in love."
"Naturally not." Peter smiled. "But be your age,
Berkley. You know whether any or all of these fellows
cared for her in that way."
"Well," said the boy with a faint half smile, "Charlie

CHAPTER IX
THERE was something strangely awful in the sudden ces-
sation of light. For an instant the three men, intent in
their various degrees on the solution of the horror so un-
compromisingly presented, seemed to have been turned
out of existence as one presses an electric switch. Peter's
breath, that had caught in his throat, passed his lips in
a silent long-drawn sigh as the crank of the camera
clicked faintly again under Wiggar's steady hand.
As sudden as the darkness, light burst, blinding upon
them. The camera was focussed on the wide hall door
that was slowly opening. Peter waited with more curi-
osity than he would have thought possible to see what
manner of man would be disclosed.
James Hayden. This, then, was he. The man who
could be suspected of making love to his father's young
wife. Who had been arrested yesterday, charged with
his father's murder.
A bold face, surely. Irregular features, cleanly cut.
A figure not above the medium in height. Broad, strong
shoulders and narrow hips. An almost military carriage.
An air of readiness and overwhelming vitality though the
face was strained and in the camera's limited palette,
painted in strong black and white.
"This happened in the dark he says." Kerrigan spoke
as if the picture were a mere continuation of the room
78
SUDDEN DEATH
79
they were in. As if James Hayden might hear him if he
raised his voice. "Of course we had to have lights on to
take it."
Kerrigan felt rather than saw his companion's nod.
The eyes of both men were glued to the figure that had
hesitated for a second in the doorway. Now Hayden
moved confidently forward, one hand stretched a little in
front of him in a gesture seeking and at the same time
protective. It was entirely the position of a person in the
dark moving among accustomed surroundings.
No fictitious drama of stage or screen had ever gripped
Peter Clancy as did this one unfolding before him. That
the man, knowing what was before him, could play his
part with any degree of self-control seemed a marvel-
and indicated-what? Hardened guilt? Or innocence?
Quietly, step by confident step, Hayden was advancing
across the room. The camera now took in a corner of
the long library table and part of the still figure on the
floor.
James Hayden's foot fell square on the butt of the
automatic, and he stopped dead. Then swiftly he stooped
and picked up the weapon. For an instant he weighed it
in his hand, paused as if in startled wonder, and moved
forward toward the lamp. In doing so his foot struck
the body.
Peter did not wonder at the awful, stony rigor of young
Hayden's face as he stooped again, feeling about the floor
in the confusion of utter horror. The leap to his feet, the
dash back across to the light switch beside the hall door
was a convincing piece of acting-if acting it was.

"The room was light from now on," whispered Ker-

With giddy rapidity the camera swung half around the
room and came to rest on the door in the back wall. Al-
though the drama was of necessity a silent one Peter felt
that the door he gazed at was opening without a sound.
Slowly it turned. Against the blackness behind her, like
some exotic flower, blossomed the figure of Emily Hay-
den. In the swift instant that she stood there Peter noted
with surprise that she was fully dressed in an embroidered
Russian tunic with hanging sleeves. A costume more
appropriate for the day than for the middle of the night.
Valentine said she had gone to bed. . . . Perhaps.
. . He would ask Kerrigan. But not now.
The camera had turned again. The flash-past of the
walls was dazzling in its swiftness. They were in front
of James Hayden now; close in front. He had whirled
away from the light switch and, still holding the auto-
matic, was staring across the room, obviously at the
woman who had at that instant opened the door.
"She called his name," Kerrigan whispered. "Low.
Like this: 'Jim!' She couldn't remember if she said any-
thing else. I asked her what she thought when she saw
him there with the pistol-and blood on his hand—and
she said she couldn't think. She didn't know what she
thought."
"She came right to him though," said Peter in a low,
wondering tone. "No hesitation about that, apparently,
for either one."

It was the end of the picture. Peter wondered for

whether it might have come when the picture was taken,
or whether it had really been there in those first awful
minutes, when the servant put his young master's—and
mistress's fate-in the hands of the police. Whatever
else there might be, Peter was certain that in it was a
look of triumph. An ugly look, evil to see if the strange
play they had just witnessed was a presentment of fact.

This time the whole awful drama moved in utter silence
across the silver screen. The effect was eerie beyond
belief. The rigidly controlled movements of the girl-
for she seemed scarcely more presented a marvel of
character and self-control-whether she was re-enacting
a heart-searing discovery, or was acting for the first time,
impromptu, a rôle charged to the full with fateful danger.
Put down in black and white. Peter considered it in-
wardly, as the explanatory scenes developed in swift
progression. In black and white, and yet-how hard to
read. As they show it, the known facts explain them-
selves-up to a point. His thoughts moved in time with
the action. The absence of fingerprints on the weapon.
. . . Impossible, that, in a case of suicide. . . . But,
if
this were the reason, a reasonable action enough. The
woman might have believed her lover's story. After what
he had seen, Peter was convinced upon this point at least.
He had no doubt now that James Hayden was madly in
love with his father's wife. Her passion for him seemed
to Peter equally obvious. And, he thought uneasily, even
if she knew better-even if she believed James Hayden
guilty of his father's death-this woman would protect
him in any case.
She was capable of much. How much? Could she—

Had Evarest tried to shoulder this crime because of a
man's devotion to another man? Damon and Pythias,
David and Jonathan. Friendship would carry a certain
type of man to almost any length. And Evarest, in his
quiet, dry, scientific way, looked the type. But Sydney
Carton did not give his life for Lucy Evremonde's hus-
band. It was his secret love for her.
Evarest had seen Emily Hayden that afternoon-and
had to all intent and purpose lied about it. There was
no other woman in that house of tragedy who, conceiv-
ably, could have left the stain of lip-rouge on that crushed
cigarette in the far end of the Hayden drawing-room.
She had gone through the dining-room and upstairs. Sim-
ple to deduce when one knew the layout of the house.

What if Evarest, a constant witness to Marvin Hayden's

Through the swift enactment of the film, Peter fought
these questions in opposing ranks within his mind. And,
for the life of him could come to no conclusion. Other
elements, other possibilities forced themselves upon him.
To give just weight to each. Not to be taken in by
beauty-and youth. The charm of personality not easily
to be withstood. Kerrigan was right. Peter knew his
own human weakness, his susceptibilities—and his nat-
ural antagonisms.

A poker face, Peter thought, very hard to read. Aloud
he said: "But you did know the provisions of your late
father's will, Mr. Hayden?"
"Valentine told you that, too?" The gray eyes nar-

of it?"
"Don't you realize that the fact that you are practically
disinherited cuts out at least one ordinary motive?" Ker-

Hayden shrugged. "I suppose that might have weight

spoke bitterly.
"Of course it will," urged Kerrigan. "And if you'd
only explain about what you told the Captain of the San
Salvador-"
"Do you believe any explanation I could make would
be worth the breath it would take?" Hayden burst out.
"I'm playing against loaded dice. My father shot him-
self. But how can I prove it?"
"I can't see for the life of me why you refuse to have
counsel," Kerrigan fumed. "It's not reasonable. The
SUDDEN DEATH
ΙΟΙ
police have got to act on what they find. And God help

Slowly Hayden broke away from Clancy's vivid glance
and turned his eyes on Kerrigan's red, angry face.
"Policemen like you don't get into the papers much, do
they, Kerrigan?" he said on a note of surprised interest.
"Damned if I don't believe you're human. Well-live-
and learn."
"Then you will get a lawyer, Mr. Hayden?"
The bold face was twisted with a queer smile. "I will
not, Captain Kerrigan. Not even for you. I'll do this
my own way." The self-willed jaw was set hard.
"That's your last word, sir?"
"At this time—and on this subject, yes, Kerrigan. It
is my last word."

With the closing of the door a strange suspense seemed
to fill the house like an odorless expanding gas. In the
almost palpable silence the moving water of the fountain
in the drawing-room was like the low sound of sobbing,
half suppressed; infinitely mournful and weirdly human,
as if it strove desperately to lose itself in the surrounding
blank stillness but could not stifle the upwelling of a tor-
tured soul. Inured to tragedy as he was, Peter felt that
120
SUDDEN DEATH
no money on earth could make him willing to live in a
house with an atmosphere like this one. To him, in a
subtle way, things as well as people were alive, and from
these paneled walls and carved balustrades and moldings
there seemed to come an emanation of something cruel
and malign. An influence that grasped and clung with
the hopeless mischief of an earth-bound spirit.
He was glad when the twilight of the stairs that they
were softly ascending gave place to the cheerful light
flooding out from his own open bedroom door. He had
been put in the front of the house and had to pass the
room of his young client, the door of which was also
open.
Peter paused and looked in. The boy was, or pre-
tended to be, asleep. He lay flung out upon his bed at
the top of the dais like a sacrifice to the gods. And a
worthy one, Peter thought, as he noted the lovely lines of
the young body, revealed rather than concealed by the
sheer texture of the thin sleeping suit. The room was
over-heated and the light bed covering swung down to
the floor in graceful classic lines. The glow from the
alabaster lamp fell impartially upon the marble figure of

"Stay here," Peter whispered, and sent his flash-light

went straight across to the one window where the cur-
tains were still parted-as they had been when Marvin
Hayden's body was discovered.
Before he closed them Peter paused an instant to gaze
out into the square. It was as if he found himself averse
to shutting out the familiar, normal world and took com-
fort in the accustomed sights and surroundings.
On the other side of the park, the lights of a taxi just
leaving the Players Club flashed flickering through the
shrubs and railings. Inside the high fence one of the
privileged owners was waiting with inevitable patience
while her little dog attended conscientiously to every bush
and tree. The same thing, even at so late an hour, may
be seen in all our city parks from the Bronx to the Bat-
SUDDEN DEATH
129
tery, a kind of dog-lovers parade. The large blue-coated
policeman that paced slowly by swinging his night-stick
was only another incident in the familiar picture, but
Peter stepped back and drew the curtains quickly and
closely together when he saw that the policeman had
turned at the corner of the pavement and was coming
slowly back. Kerrigan had thought it worth while to
place a guard outside the house as well as in then. In
that case it was necessary to be extra careful about lights.
If one essays an experiment, however altruistic, without
the required legal authority it is more than advisable not
to involve the sturdy blue arm of the law and the night-
stick that adds so many inches to its length.
Satisfied that the heavy cornice and draperies would

He knelt there on the chair for a minute looking out.

The rest of the slender shape was dim against the dark-

one but Emily Hayden in that house could look and move
like that.
Steadily, without faltering, she came down the stairs.
The rear hall was only less dark than the dining-room
but Peter knew she was moving directly towards them.
Guiding Wiggar with the lightest touch, Peter stepped

CHAPTER XVII
THE two went directly into the dining-room and Mrs.
Hayden seated herself at a small secretary in the corner.
Her brother remained by her side. There was a strange
cold aura of isolation about the pair, as if in their own
minds they had been suddenly set apart from the life
interests of their fellows. And yet the girl was carrying
on as if this day were to be like all the days that had
passed. The servants came in for orders as was obviously
the daily habit. Peter caught a glimpse of them through
the door as he followed Kerrigan out into the hall.
The assistant district attorney was just leaving. He
stopped for a few minutes to talk importantly to Ker-
rigan, and a little later pompously departed taking In-
verness with him.
In the meantime, Peter went swiftly and silently up
to his own room. Wiggar was there putting the finishing
touches to a bowl of narcissus and yellow freesia that he
had wangled from downstairs. The room looked extraor-
dinarily sunny and cheerful. Peter smiled his approba-
tion but wasted no words.
"Where's Gillespie, do you know?" he asked in a low
voice.
"He's overseeing the work below stairs, sir. A thor-

own line, I can assure you, Mr. Peter."

CHAPTER XVIII
BUSINESS was going on as usual in the Homicide Court
that April morning. The judge sat at an ordinary desk
on a raised platform before which was a railing that might
easily have been that of an ordinary office except for
the row of seats like cheap theatre stalls that were backed
against it. Between them and the dais was a long table,
a few chairs, and a little desk at which an absorbed sten-
ographer worked steadily; never raising his eyes from
the spot of light thrown down upon his note book and
his flying pencil.
Across the window back of the "bench" the Stars and
Stripes were nailed up quite casually and a little crooked.
There was just one thing that gave distinction to the
place; the white head of the judge, shining above his
black silk robe and boldly silhouetted against the flag
that was his symbol of authority.
In the brief pause occasioned by the stir of their en-
trance, the judge nodded slightly to Kerrigan and, with
a faint twinkle of the eye, to Peter. Though the latter
avoided the limelight as much as possible, they two had
met before.
"If order is not maintained in this court-" the judge's
voice was raised a little though he spoke without heat,
"—the public will be excluded." He looked severely at
several newspaper men who had surged forward and they
175
176
SUDDEN DEATH
quickly subsided. Judge Carstairs was known as a man
of his word, and even the Press took notice.
Emily Hayden looked swiftly about her as Kerrigan
found places for them in one of the dozen or so rows of
seats at the rear of the room. Peter was not the only
one who knew that she was looking for James Hayden.
Valentine was quick to notice the expression of her face
and to Evarest also it was eloquent of an almost unbear-
able conflict between longing and dread. From his posi-
tion near the door Peter could watch them all and he
found it in his heart to pity Evarest especially for sud-
denly he caught a glimpse of the real man as the scientist
put up his handkerchief apparently to stifle a fit of
coughing that shook his spare shoulders. He was calm
again almost instantly and turned to regard with strict
intelligence the to him unaccustomed procedure in the
development of the case of a drunken motorist and a
fatal accident that was then being heard.
Police officers, witnesses, and defendant followed each
other in the square witness box that stood on the judge's
left. After what seemed an interminable time, though it
was really only a short half hour, the prosecutor said,
"That's the case, your honor." After which the judge's
voice, strong and stern. "I direct that a complaint be
drawn-" The erstwhile defendant rose dizzily and was
conducted by two police officers out through a steel-
meshed door that shut heavily behind his sagging figure.
Followed a subdued murmur and a quick, almost noise-
less shifting of places. The air seemed suddenly to
thicken as if electrically charged. As quietly as possible
SUDDEN DEATH
177
the camera men prepared their machines. The door to
the detention room swung open, there was a blinding
flash of light and the sound of grinding film-reels. Ac-
companied by an officer James Hayden came quickly into
the room and was guided to his place at the low table
facing the judge.
There had been only an instant of time when the wit-
nesses and the accused man had faced each other as he
was hurried through the gate in the heavy railing, but
the eyes of Emily Hayden had conveyed their message
of passionate devotion. Evarest had managed to keep
between her and the newspaper men while they moved
to places inside the rail and Valentine Berkley had ably
seconded his efforts. Peter, seeing everything, felt for
the boy an odd mixture of sympathy and understanding
slightly touched with amused contempt. If the papers
wished, they would be able to show some good pictures of
Valentine's handsome profile.
In a few seconds they were all seated. An expectant
quiet had fallen on the room. Emily Hayden looked
about fearfully for Updegraff, the assistant district at-
torney, who had caused so much anguish of soul on the
morning following her husband's death, but he was no-
where to be seen. She could not understand this and
there was no opportunity to ask what his absence might
betoken.
Inverness was there and the butler, Gillespie, with some
police officers in the row of seats against the railing on

attorney's office who had represented the People in the
178
SUDDEN DEATH
preceding case was already presenting this one to the

but seemed remarkably familiar with the various aspects
of the case which he enumerated clearly but in so low a
tone as to render his words indistinguishable to those

The judge listened, leaning sidewise, his clear bright
little blue eyes fixed on a point in space. There was in-
telligence in his high forehead and benignity as well as
strength in his firm lips and chin. "A bit of luck having
Judge Carstairs on the bench," Peter whispered to Ker-

mind," growled Kerrigan. "Independent as a kid on
roller skates. But thorough. . . . Wylie, from the pros-
ecutor's office, is a good egg, too. You watch. Here
we go."
"Defendant refuses counsel. Pleads not guilty and
ready, your honor."
Peter knew from Valentine Berkley that young Hayden
had been forced by his father to study law, but even so
he was surprised at the man's apparently firm grasp of the
situation. He sat there alone, facing the judge with a
confidence that gave no hint of bravado. The judge, him-
self a gentleman, could hardly fail to be impressed, Peter
thought, with the other's attitude.
The routine went rapidly forward. Hayden agreed to
the stipulations as to a possible subsequent trial, and the
acceptance of the medical officer's testimony that Marvin
Hayden, deceased, had died from the effects of a bullet
wound in the head. The arresting officer was sworn,
SUDDEN DEATH
179
made his brief report and removed his magnificent blue-
clothed frame from the witness box.
The prosecuting attorney leaned across, said something
quietly to the judge then stepped off the platform and
spoke to Inverness. Everything was done with a queer
lack of formality that somehow only served to increase
the dread in the breast of Emily Hayden as she realized
that she was now being called to the witness stand. The
flash of camera bulbs added horribly to her confusion and
she had to call up every ounce of courage she possessed
in order to keep from fainting as she seated herself facing
those cruel moving "eyes of the world."
With a stern glance at the men of the Press over whom
his power was necessarily limited, Judge Carstairs turned
courteously to this girl of his own class, who might have
been a friend of his daughter and who was obviously near
the limit of endurance.
"Please don't be frightened, Mrs. Hayden," he said in
a drawling voice that hinted of a youth spent under sun-
nier skies. "We only want to find out what actually hap-
pened. There is no animus of any kind tolerated in this
court."
He looked straight at the prosecutor as he said this.
Wylie smiled inwardly. Judge Carstairs cramped some
people's style, but not his. He felt he could get facts out
of witnesses without bashing them over the head. Time
enough for that later-if the case went to the Grand
Jury-which seemed on the face of it-probable.
Somewhat surprised but not a little calmed and
strengthened by the judge's manner, Emily Hayden felt
180
SUDDEN DEATH
her courage surging upward. She took the simple oath
in a sweet, firm voice and at the Court's request told her
story clearly and with apparent sincerity. To Peter,
closely listening, it seemed as if he were again witnessing,
from a darkened room, the tragic event as pictured on
Kerrigan's film. There was not a slip on a single detail.
When she had finished the prosecutor asked several
questions without bringing out anything that Peter did
not already know. The beauty of the quiet figure in the
witness box and the quality of her exquisite voice made
a strong appeal. Wylie's tone was quite sympathetic and
friendly as he asked: "Now, Mrs. Hayden, if you had
heard nothing to alarm you and if you thought your hus-

"I could not sleep," she said, turning her great eyes
upon the judge's thoughtful face. "A new Reader's
Digest had come. You know it has short articles. I had

I opened the door-" She stopped and her lids closed as
if to shut out an unbearable recollection.
Judge Carstairs said quietly: "You were surprised
when you saw the defendant?”
Her look flashed full on James Hayden whose upturned

strangely in contrast with the girl's vehement protest.

"Yes. He told me. It was business."

Her eyes still rested on James Hayden and her hesita-

This time she answered promptly and clearly. "Yes."
The prosecution asked a few more questions that elic-

there was, of course, no opportunity for them to exchange
so much as a word.
Immediately afterwards, Gillespie was called. He had

not falter in his testimony, throughout which his bitter
enmity toward his late master's wife and son was strik-

any effort of the prosecution to further develop the serv-
ant's scarcely veiled innuendoes.
"We are not interested in opinions. Only in facts."
The judge looked hard at the prosecuting attorney.
"That's true, your honor. But I understood the wit-

SUDDEN DEATH
ness to state that he actually heard a quarrel or quar-

CHAPTER XIX
As Wylie stepped back, Gillespie, with a sudden fierce

was careful to keep his judicial mind open and on an

back to New York hoping to join Señor Velasquez again
in the morning, or if I was delayed, I would still have

CHAPTER XX
FOR a minute the court room was so still that it might
have been inhabited only by disembodied spirits. Judge

Marvin Hayden handed his gun to his son and asked him

"Rather sounds as if you were summing up for the de-

The judge took off his glasses and passed his hand

dling of the weapon involved, together with medical testi-
mony favoring a theory of suicide, and, in addition, the
fact that defendant knew that he would lose his present
maintenance and would inherit practically nothing in the
event of his father's death- Defendant moves that the
case be dismissed."
A strained pause, and the judge added quietly. "Mo-
tion granted. Dismissed."

The sympathetic uproar in the court room was tre-

Reporters surged forward, while blue-coated officers, like

Never in all her life would Emily Hayden forget those
minutes: the look of authority and confidence in the face
of the judge when Jim had tried to thank him: the pres-
sure of Jim's hand once more in hers, his eyes-seeking—
seeking her own trying to show him all the depths of
the love that was in her heart. It was worth it. Worth
everything. Free. Free-both of them-and all the
roads of the world open. What was that old refrain?

CHAPTER XXI
WIGGAR served Peter's luncheon in his delightfully ap-
pointed apartment as if there was nothing more serious
in life to be considered than the arrangement of the
flowers upon the table.
"You don't think the color of the delphinium, sir, is a
trifle too strong for the narcissus and daffodils and the
pink tulips?"
"I approve of your color scheme in toto," Peter smiled.
"In that flat green bowl they make me feel like Pan be-
side a sylvan lake. Only I doubt if Pan ever tasted
mushroom sous cloche. I'll have a little more, Wiggar."
Peter even took a short rest after lunch which filled
Wiggar's cup to the brim, for when the nap was com-
pleted the cold, which was its partial excuse, was prac-
tically gone.
"Now I'm for it," said Peter, "and I won't need you
until later in the afternoon. I'll phone. If anyone wants
to get in touch with me, I'm going first to my own office,
then to see Captain Kerrigan, and when I'm sure that
awful funeral is over I'll probably go back to Gramercy
Park."
"Shall I be fetching your things from there, sir?”
Peter was conscious of the valet's grave scrutiny. "I-
don't know," the master replied. "I have a feeling,
Wiggar, that we may be requested to remain.”

Following his plan, Peter spent some time in con-

own office, where his appearance, owing to its infrequency,
usually caused a general ripple of excitement. This was no
exception to the rule for he had communicated with them

and afterward as need arose. He found that the interest
aroused was more than satisfactory and that the various
lines of inquiry he had suggested were being or had been
followed through and he left the office wiser on several
possibly important points.
He then hastened down to Kerrigan's office and was
fortunate enough to find that able and conscientious per-

between them Kerrigan said:
"No, I'm not surprised either. Why should I be, know-

Peter sighed. "Maybe a barn door or two, but I flatter

His thoughts took a sudden turn to more definite con-

crossed the street at a round pace and went off along the
north side of Gramercy Park.
Peter had been in plain sight, but whether he had been
recognized or not he had no means of telling. He quick-
ened his pace to a run and was in time to see Hayden
walking quietly westward and, sure enough, there was the
man in the black derby, following on with a technique
that was, compared to Peter's, as the work of a smart
mongrel to that of a dog of the "Seeing Eye."

CHAPTER XXII
PETER wondered how Gillespie could come back and serve
the dinner that night, and nothing said. The old butler
went about his task in the great dining-room as if noth-
ing had occurred to disturb its peace. There was an un-
mistakable significance, however, in the fact that in the
master's place at the head of the table, he had arranged a
magnificent bouquet of white flowers in a huge silver urn
flanked by candles. The rest of the table was laid out in
conventional elegance and a very good dinner was very
well served.
It was scarcely to be wondered at, however, that Valen-
tine and Peter were the only ones who did justice to the
repast and it was on them that the burden of conversation
fell. James Hayden kept up his end well, all things con-
sidered, but Mrs. Hayden had been visibly staggered by
the appearance of the table when she entered, and had
found sufficient courage only to ignore it. The son of the
house had turned first white and then red, but after a
glance at the lovely woman on his arm had refrained
from comment. Valentine thought aloud that it was very
artistic and no doubt appropriate but not cheerful some-
how, and he liked flowers that weren't so tall and put in
the middle where they belonged.
"There will be a change to-morrow, sir." Gillespie
bowed his white head in acquiescence and spoke in a per-
221
222
SUDDEN DEATH
fectly colorless voice, but Peter thought Emily Hayden
shuddered, though she tried to respond bravely to her
brother's smile.
During dinner, Peter was called away from the table to
speak to a messenger who insisted on seeing him in per-
son. He apologized cheerfully to his hostess when he
returned but later contrived to slip a tiny note, unper-
ceived, into young Hayden's hand.
The evening passed quietly and agreeably enough.
The present mistress of the house may have thought it odd
that Valentine's new friend lingered on and on, but she
was more than grateful to him for the succession of in-
teresting adventures he was able to put before them in a
vivid though modest way. It relieved her of all effort, and
his strong resourceful voice soothed her into a quietness
she would not have believed possible, for the day had been
a succession of varied strains upon her well guarded
nerves. She made it an excuse for retiring at an early
hour. Valentine went up with her, and without effort on
his part, James Hayden found it possible to accede to the
request on the slip of paper that had been thrust into his
hand.
As soon as they were alone, "Well, Mr. Clancy," he
said, "here I am. You wanted to have a talk with me.
All right. I'm ready." His manner was braced and
expectant.
"Let's go over there." Peter indicated a far corner of
the drawing-room. "We can see-and we can't be heard."
"As bad as that." Hayden smiled but there was no
mirth in his sharp glance. "You found something new
SUDDEN DEATH
223
and interesting in the message you received while we were
at dinner?"
"It was news to me, but not to you, Mr. Hayden."
Peter's face was very grave as he sat down close by and
lighted a cigarette. "I don't wish to force your confi-
dence, but I beg you to consider seriously the fact that
if I can find out a thing, so can the police."
"Sooner or later. I suppose that's right. But I fancy
your mind is a bit more nimble than your competitors',
or colleagues' or what have you."
Peter passed the compliment with a curt gesture. "I
have become very much interested in this case, Hayden,"
he said. "And I find myself in a difficulty. I am, myself,
so full of original sin that a holier-than-thou attitude is
hard to maintain and my sympathy is too often with the
defendant, even in some rare instances when I know he is
a criminal. Fortunately, or unfortunately, as the case
may be, there are generally innocent persons to be con-
sidered and the duty to them naturally comes first."
"Which adds up," Hayden suggested, "to what?”
"I want to know exactly how strong the case is against
you," Peter replied with sober earnestness. "To me it

CHAPTER XXV
PETER's ring was answered almost instantly. It was the
old superior servant Parker that opened the door, but
he had only started to relieve the late guest of hat and
coat when Douglas Evarest came quickly out into the hall.

ishness is a strong armor, and when consistently worn

electrical apparatus. Above it was an elaborate switch-
board and open fuse box, marked for the house connec-
tions of power, light and heat.

After a second Peter went on with steady purpose.

Hayden was cured-forever let us hope-of the incipient
madness that was wrecking several lives and of which I
am certain the man who fired that fatal shot was well
aware. At that minute of exaltation I believe he had
little fear of consequences. There was no alarm after
the shot. The house, beyond those sound-proof walls,
remained as it had been-silent. . . . He waited-to
make sure. Then he bent over the body. Saw that the
work was finished. Pulled out a handkerchief, wiped the
butt of the pistol, pressed the dead hand about it, and
left it there. Suicide. Plain and incontrovertible. That
was the only solution he believed possible, as he switched
off the light, let himself out of the house, and went away
without being seen by a living soul."

CHAPTER XXVI
"BUT how can I prove it?" Evarest started up holding
both clenched hands against the sides of his neck. “I've
put it out of my power. God help me, Clancy. I should
have known. No man, ultimately, escapes the conse-
quences of his own acts- And to have Jim suspected—
and Emily-in danger-is worse, far worse than death.
From the minute I heard of Jim's return, I've literally
suffered the tortures of the damned. When they arrested
him-I began at once to make preparations to take over.
I knew that Jim, as soon as he understood, would forgive
me-for everything. And in addition to all the rest, even
for the few hours' necessary delay. You see, Clancy, I
had no idea, then, that anything would be required more
than a simple statement of my guilt."
"Yes. I know," said Peter. "I saw that. I suspected
at that time that you might possibly be telling the truth,
but I could not quite understand why you had delayed
matters for over twenty-four hours."
"There were things that I could not leave unfinished."

SUDDEN DEATH
thought it inadvisable to let purely personal considera-

"And is due to my own criminal folly," Evarest replied
with a passionate gesture. "She refused to believe what
I told her, and she will never admit even to herself now
that I deceived her in the first instance. I know her.
Nothing on earth will induce her to question the evidence
of her own senses, as she puts it, and one has no right
to blame her.”
"Sometimes we build not wisely but too well," said

Peter appeared to be examining the clever contrivance
on the wall. In reality he was arranging his thoughts,
swiftly, carefully, in logical sequence. He must hold out
no false hopes. Evarest's mother would be adamant.
The son was obviously right in believing that she would
refuse to untie his hands. Who knew better than he did
that, whatever he might force her to understand of the

suited to her purpose. And her manner would be per-

petent charming side that she was able to show to be sure

He had guided Evarest to a chair by the small open

The stricken man, after a swift clasp of the hand, held

Right breast pocket, one handkerchief, folded, clean.

They talked long then on ways and means. The ob-

a time when my conscience was in accord with the doing

"But at last I have seen that I was wrong. The terror

A strange note had come into the quiet voice that went
calmly on and on. Peter had a feeling of exaltation in
witnessing such serene courage and swore within himself
that he would do his best for this splendid though guilty
soul.
"I believe now," said Douglas Evarest in the same
still voice, "that there is no such thing as righteous indig-
nation-no occasion when passion can be taken as a guide
to justice. For that mistake I have suffered and must
suffer. But I am not willing to have that the end. I am
not convinced that my crime was so great that my death
-or whatever punishment will be meted out—must be
nothing but an expiation, and can serve no constructive
purpose.
""
He rose then, and half turned away, he said: "Do you

SUDDEN DEATH
you a notice in the paper?"
Peter's heart contracted.
Suddenly he knew-knew
what was coming. But he could say nothing.
"Dr. Lauren, it said," the calm voice went on, "needed
only one thing to make sure his serum was effective. It
was practically impossible to get human beings to take
the risk of leprosy."
"Don't!" Peter put out his hand. "Don't-"
"It is already done," said Douglas Evarest tranquilly.
"I persuaded Dr. Lauren after I left you. I've had the
first inoculation. Now whatever happens, I am content.
I will die-sometime, in any case. If I live now-I live.
If I die being guilty of death-I will have given my
body in an effort to overcome a death more hideous.
Strange. Strange how one law seems to supersede and
make use of lesser laws. If I had been-innocent-I
would not have done this. Strange. Perhaps Marvin's
death was a necessary part of the sequence. Perhaps―
neither he-nor I-will have died in vain."
He lifted his face. Peter beheld it transfigured.
Beauty. Love. Truth. He saw them all together at last.
Built up of sorrow, pain and sin, and triumphant over
them.
Raising his eyes he saw that the sky above their heads
was whitening to the dawn of a new day.
